# Bridge — Art Direction

This file is the single source of truth for the **bridge app's** visual style — applies to `apps/bridge/` only, not to any other Becknology app. All procedural drawing AND all PNG asset generation for the bridge must conform to this spec. If a new asset doesn't fit these rules, the asset is wrong, not the rules.

> **Agents (Claude Code, Codex, etc.):** copy/paste prompts live in `apps/bridge/PNG-PROMPTS.md`. The full master prompt is also embedded in this file under "PNG generation rules" so either is a valid source.

<!-- Last reviewed: 2026-10-14 -->

## North star
**Our own little model world, finished like toys, in soft light** (Beck's call after Look Book 2, October 2026: "toy, the
normal toy, not HD. With soft light… the normal faces… make a face for them for that picture, kind of like Stardew").
Everything is the game's own 3D models rendered by Blender at 32 px a tile — the 45° camera, the chunky cel-shaded
people, the scene kits — and then finished as toys:

- **The toy finish.** Light falls in a few flat bands (no smooth gradients, no dithering), shadows lean cool and the
  brightest light warm, and a strong dark line runs wherever one thing stands in front of another — round a house, a
  tree, a barrel, a person — but never inside the ground, between a roof's shingles or round a blade of grass. Not HD:
  32 px a tile, as ever.
- **Soft light.** What gives off light glows into its surroundings (js/gpu.js bloom, from each scene's glow image) and
  the frame's corners settle a little darker (the vignette), so lamps, windows and neon read as light, not paint.
- **Live light.** Outdoors the sun really crosses the sky with the planet's clock: shadows swing from the left in the
  morning to the right in the evening and lie long and warm at golden hour, the moon and the lamps take over at night;
  the seasons and the weather work on the same picture (snow on whatever faces up, autumn trees each their own colour,
  wet ground in the rain) and still water mirrors its far bank (js/litscene.js, *Live light* below).
- **Places with shape and life.** Land that rises and falls, terraces, banks sloping into water; vegetation in layers;
  buildings from the richer house kit (`assets_house.py`); small life at the human scale everywhere. The bar is the
  reference dioramas in `tools/bridge-art/dioramas/` (Millpond Farm, Lantern Quay, the Hoarfell tarn, Elderglade Brook,
  Rainline Street) — "swing larger… still in our 3D style… less boxy and thrown together".
- **People with faces.** The same chibi models with a simple drawn face — two eyes with a catch-light, brows, a mouth, a
  touch of blush (the "normal" face, not the round one) — and, when you talk to someone, a Stardew-style portrait,
  drawn: a real face, hair and shoulders, not a toy's round head (*Characters — the Blender pipeline*: faces are
  painted; *Portraits*).

Stardew Valley and Cast n Chill are still the touchstones for coziness, readability and constant small motion (smoke,
flicker, water, breathing idles); the look is ours. Older art (AI-painted PNGs, procedural drawers, scenes rendered
before the toy finish) is being brought over — the backlog is in `_claude_docs/requests.md` (*The new look*). **Every
new place is made the new way** (*Making a new place* below).

## Rendering pipeline (js/world.js)

1. Each frame the world is painted into a small offscreen canvas at **32 native px per tile**, then scaled to the screen by a **whole number** of device pixels (`zoom`). Never draw world art straight to the screen — everything lands on the same pixel grid this way.
2. The camera snaps the world to whole native pixels; the leftover fraction shifts the finished frame, so scrolling stays smooth.
3. Draw order: background → tiles (static ones from a cache) → glow → tileset overlay → loot → **pilot** → tall sprites whose base is below the pilot (so the pilot can walk behind buildings) → lighting → effects → screen-space UI (exit beacons, game markers, vignette — crisp text at CSS resolution).
4. Static tiles are detected automatically at world load (drawn at several sample times; tiles whose pixels change, or that paint outside their cell, stay live) and cached. A few cached rows are refreshed every frame, so state-driven drawers still update. Call `BridgeWorld.invalidateTiles()` after changing what a static tile looks like.
5. Movement, camera and animation run on real elapsed time (`dt`), never frame counts.
6. Inside a background or overlay drawer, use `BridgeWorld.getView()` (`{offX, offY, ts, w, h}` in native px) — not `getScale()`/`getCamera()`, which describe the screen. For mouse picking / DOM placement use `BridgeWorld.getScreenView()` (CSS px).
7. The main canvas's size and scale are set every frame (`js/main.js`): a window moved to another screen changes its pixel density without a resize, and a canvas the browser drops and hands back (short of graphics memory) comes back unscaled — on `contextrestored` the scene composites and sea textures are rebuilt too.

## Resolution & scale

| Concept | Value |
|---|---|
| Native tile | 32 × 32 px (`ts` passed to every drawer) |
| World data tile | `tileSize: 16` in world JSON is a logical unit — don't change it |
| Procedural unit `u` | `ts / 16` = 2 native px (procedural art is still authored on a 16-unit grid) |
| Sprite density | 32 native px per tile — 1 native px of detail |
| Screen zoom | whole number of device px per native px, chosen so ~20 tiles fit across |
| All scaling | nearest-neighbor only — `imageSmoothingEnabled = false` (except the light map, which is soft on purpose) |

## Procedural drawing rules — match the PNG style
Code-drawn things that are still in the game (creatures, crops, effects, small props) should sit with the toy finish: flat tones, no gradients on subjects, a dark outline.

The procedural drawers in `js/worlds/*.js` should look like they were drawn at 16 logical pixels per tile, even though they're rendered with `fillRect`. This is what keeps procedural and PNG art looking like the same family.

**Rules:**
1. **No sub-pixel detail.** Every `fillRect` width/height should be a whole multiple of `u` (or at minimum `Math.max(1, u)` for thin lines). Avoid `u * 0.4`, `u * 0.5`, etc. — those are vector-style.
2. **Limited palette per element.** 3-4 tones max: highlight, midtone, shadow, deep shadow. No smooth gradients in foreground objects (gradients are OK for sky/sea/halo backgrounds where we want a soft fade).
3. **Hard edges.** Pixel-perfect color boundaries. No anti-aliasing.
4. **1-pixel outlines on subjects.** Most foreground objects should have a `Math.max(1, u)` dark outline — matches Stardew and PNG style.
5. **Animation as overlay.** Animation goes in a separate pass on top of static art (see `drawChimneySmoke`, `drawWindowFlicker`). Keep it cheap and frame-based — small additive sprites or simple `Math.sin` modulations.

## PNG generation rules
**Legacy.** New art is built in Blender in the game's own kit (*Making a new place*); no new AI-painted PNGs. These rules stay for the PNG sprites still in the game until they're replaced.


**View angle: high oblique elevated front-facing RPG building view.** The building faces directly forward with the full front facade readable, AND most or nearly all of the roof plane is visible from above. The roof should feel almost top-down while the front wall stays fully visible. This is the classic farming-RPG building sprite that drops onto a top-down tile map (Stardew, Harvest Moon, Rune Factory).

**Not allowed:** true isometric, diagonal rotation, realistic vanishing-point perspective, side view, or pure top-down roof-only view.

**Composition:**
- Door at bottom-center so the building sits naturally on a tile map.
- Roof plane visible from above at a strong high oblique — most/all of the roof readable.
- Front facade fully readable below the roof.
- Windows are clearly defined frames with simple yellow-glow glass — *no detail painted inside the window*. We layer animated glow on top.
- Chimney (if present) at upper-right or upper-left of the roof, with empty space *above* the chimney top. We draw smoke procedurally there.
- Lantern (if present) beside the door, with a small flame shape. We replace the flame with animated procedural flicker.
- Hanging sign (if present) attached to a corner of the upper floor by a chain/bracket.
- Stone or wooden foundation visible at the base, sitting on the ground.

**Master prompt (paste into every PNG generation — long form):**

> Create a pixel art PNG of a [BUILDING TYPE] for a cozy 2D farming RPG.
>
> **View / camera.** Use a consistent elevated front-facing RPG building view. The building faces directly forward. Show the full front facade clearly. Show most or nearly all of the roof plane from above, with a strong high oblique angle. The roof should feel almost top-down while the front wall remains fully readable. This should look like a classic farming RPG building sprite placed on a top-down map. Keep the entrance near the bottom-center so the building sits naturally on a tile map.
>
> **Perspective rules.** No true isometric perspective. No diagonal rotation. No realistic vanishing-point perspective. No side view. No pure top-down roof-only view. The angle should be a fixed high oblique building sprite view.
>
> **Pixel art style.** Chunkier, more visibly pixelated high-resolution pixel art. Strong visible pixel blocks and pixel clusters. Crisp hard edges. No anti-aliasing. No smooth painting. No blur. No realism. No 3D, no voxel, no low-poly. Clear dark outlines. Slightly simplified forms so the sprite reads cleanly. Cozy retro RPG aesthetic. Warm, inviting lighting. Clean sprite-like shading. Readable at small size.
>
> **Visual language / consistency.** Same camera angle, roof visibility, pixel density, chunky pixel treatment, outline thickness, cozy farming-RPG mood, relative scale, and simplified clean sprite rendering as the rest of the building set.
>
> **Composition.** Center the building in the canvas. Show the full building with minimal transparent padding. Transparent background. Clear silhouette. Keep the building isolated as a clean game asset sprite.
>
> **Design details.** [INSERT BUILDING DETAILS HERE]
>
> **Match the same camera angle, roof visibility, outline weight, and chunkier pixel-art rendering as the existing building set.**

**Master prompt — short form (single paragraph):**

> Create a pixel art PNG of a [BUILDING TYPE] for a cozy 2D farming RPG. Use a fixed elevated front-facing building view: show the full front facade, but also show most or nearly all of the roof from above, as if viewed at a high oblique angle. The roof should feel almost top-down while the front wall is still fully visible. No isometric view, no diagonal rotation, no realistic perspective, and no pure top-down roof-only view. Use chunky, visibly pixelated high-resolution pixel art with crisp hard edges, strong pixel blocks, dark outlines, simple clean sprite shading, no anti-aliasing, no smoothing, transparent background, minimal padding, and a warm cozy farming-RPG aesthetic. Keep the camera angle, roof visibility, pixel density, and overall sprite style consistent with the rest of the building set. Design details: [INSERT DETAILS]. Match the same camera angle, roof visibility, outline weight, and chunkier pixel-art rendering as the existing building set.

**Sizing reference (native PNG dimensions):**
| Asset type | Footprint (tiles) | Native PNG dimensions |
|---|---|---|
| Small house / cottage | 3w × 3h | 768 × 768 |
| Medium building (tavern, inn) | 4w × 4-5h | 768 × 768 to 1024 × 1024 |
| Large building (manor) | 5w × 5h | 1024 × 1024 |
| Tall tower / lighthouse | 2-3w × 5-7h | 384-512 × 1024+ |
| Wide creature (dragon) | 4w × 3h | 1024 × 768 |
| Hero sign / billboard | 4w × 2h | 1024 × 512 |

Keep the subject's real proportions — the sprite is no longer stretched to fill its footprint, so a square image of a 4-wide building comes out 4 tiles tall. **Native resolution is high (700-1200px) on purpose** — `tools/bridge-art/native_sprites.py` downsamples to 32 px per tile. Don't ask an image model for "low res pixel art at 64px"; it doesn't render small images well. Generate big, convert with the tool.

**Animation hooks.** When generating PNGs, design them so we can layer animation on top:
- **Chimney**: leave clear sky directly above the chimney top — no overhang or roof piece extending up there. Smoke needs vertical room.
- **Windows**: paint a flat warm yellow rectangle in the frame. Don't paint candles, faces, or detail inside the window. We pulse the window with a procedural overlay.
- **Door**: paint a closed door, rectangular and simple. We may overlay an "open" frame later.
- **Lantern by door**: paint the lantern body but the flame area should be a small empty circle/oval so we can flicker it procedurally.
- **Hanging sign**: chain from the building, with the sign hanging straight down (we may add a sway animation later).

### Verdance — the farm world, in daylight
- Meadow: `#5a8a3c` (lit ~`#689a40`), hedgerow `#3a6a30`, pines `#2e5a38`
- Earth: bare field `#8c6037` (lit), tilled `#68442a` with furrows `#4e321c`, wet `#46301e` / `#2e1e12`
- Lane and yards: `#9a7a4e`; timber `#7a5a38`, barn red, whitewash `#efe2c6`, roofs `#a04a30` and `#3a6a3a`
- Crops (js/farm.js, 16-unit art): leaf `#4a8a2a` `#7ac040` `#2e5a1c`, outline `#1c2c12`; produce in its own colours (turnip `#a050a0`/`#f0eaf0`, carrot `#f08020`, strawberry `#e02a3a`, sunflower `#f8c820`, pumpkin `#f08a20`, starfruit `#ffe040`)
- Dialogs: cream linen `#f2ecd6` in a green gingham frame, title `#3a7a2a`
- The one world under a sun: one key light from the west (`k.sun`), the sky's blue in the shadows, no flicker
- **Brambleford** (`verdance-village`, `tools/bridge-art/scenes/verdance_village.py`, kit `assets_village.py`): the farm town, where the shuttle sets down (`BridgeShip` LANDING, its own landing green with the parked skiff layer). Every building its own: the Crusty Loaf (cream and terracotta, bread in the window; Bridget sells bread, honey cake, pumpkin pie), the Hayloft (a thatched two-storey inn; Mags — ale, cider, stew, a ploughman's), Pennyworth's (brick and slate, a striped awning over windows of jars; Percy — seeds and sundries), St Elm's chapel and its bell tower, the Anvil & Hoof (an open forge, a live hearth; Wat — the Lucky Horseshoe, farm tools), Ludd's watermill on the stream, cottages behind picket fences, the allotments and Nan Ashby's over the stone bridge. Names are painted up in real lettering (`V.board_sign`, the farm-town faces Rye, IM Fell English SC and Alegreya SC in `tools/bridge-art/fonts`), plus the old bracket signs. The square has the well (fills the can), Nell's flower stall (posies — a gift anyone likes), the notice board, the pub's tables and the old oak. Villagers walk the street (`walkers.json`: the old farmer, the schoolmistress, a villager); each shopkeeper talks (`people.json`) and has tastes (`js/gifts.js`). The seasons regrade it with the farm and the rain falls on both.
- **The farm is off the town:** Meadow Lane leaves Brambleford south over the footbridge (`enter_world` at the map's foot) and comes in at the farm's east edge by Hollin's (and back). The farm has no landing pad any more — hay is stacked in the top meadow where it was — and no `shuttle` key; Return to ship from the farm just fades.

- **The elves** (Beck: "make those people more unique and look like elves... what elf fits"): every one has the ears (slim, swept up and back past the hair — Alyssa found the first, longer ones read as Shrek's), long hair (flowing, braided, tied) and an outfit of their own — recognisable at a glance, each a different silhouette and colour:
  - Queen Alyssara (`npc-alyssa`, seated): very fair, long flowing blonde hair ending in points, a gold circlet with a tall gold leaf holding an emerald and small leaves at the temples, a court gown (`gown: 'court'`) in a pale, luminous green with long bell sleeves cuffed in gold, a gold V down the bodice to an emerald pendant, gold at the shoulders, the gold girdle's end hanging down the skirt, a gold panel down the front, a double gold hem and gold leaves worked into the skirt; seated, the skirt falls over her lap and down to the floor in one sweep (no legs); a sheer ivory-gold mantle (`veil`) from her shoulders to the floor behind; gold slippers. (Alyssa: more opulent, more ethereal, more gold, and it should read as a dress.)
  - Beckolas (`npc-beckolas`): blond, tied back, with a thin braid from each temple (a gold bead at the end), blue eyes; leaf-green armour — a breastplate of leaf scales with a gold leaf on the chest, pauldrons of three leaf plates a side — over a leaf-cut tunic, a long leaf-hemmed cloak with a leaf mantle and a gold clasp, bark-brown bracers and tall elven boots; the sword at his left hip, the longbow and quiver worn over the cloak. Every other leaf (scales, pauldron plates, the cloak's hem and mantle) is a brighter green (`leaf_light`) so the leaves read as leaves.
  - Aerin the warden (`npc-elf-warden`): a pointed forest-green hood up, the ears out through it, a leaf-cut capelet, a leather jerkin over a moss tunic, a long leaf cloak, bracers, tall boots, the longbow held upright in the left hand, a quiver on the back, auburn hair.
  - Thaelin the bard (`npc-elf-bard`, seated): autumn russet, a green cap slouched to one side with a long pale feather, long dark hair, a lute across his knees.
  - Mirelle the herbalist (`npc-elf-herbalist`): white hair in one long braid over her shoulder tied with lavender, a crown of the Moonwell's flowers, an apron of leaves in two greens, an herb satchel with sprigs poking out.
  - Faelan the lamplighter (`npc-elf-lamplighter`): an elder — silver hair, a twilight-blue robe trimmed in gold, a moss hood fallen back, a tall bark staff with a lit leaf lantern hung from its crook.
  - Sefa the weaver (`npc-elf-weaver`): copper hair in two braids down her front and a braid wound round her crown, heather violet, a Moonwell-green shawl cut into leaves, a basket of gold, green and silver wool on her arm.
  - Pip (`npc-elf-child`): child-sized, chestnut hair under a crown of leaves with gold and russet flowers, a new-leaf tunic.
- **The elves' options** in `characters.py` (shared, so any future elf can wear them; all built from the same cel-shaded primitives through `spike` (a cone from a base along a direction, its cross-section flattened into a blade), `rod`, `chain` (beads along a line: braids), `stave` (a bent bow), `leaf_ring` (leaf points round a hem), `drape` (a flared cloak of panels) and `leaf_mantle`): `elf_ears` (long blades swept up and back past the hair — in profile the far one is left out, `EARS`, or it reads as a horn), `circlet` (+ `gem`), `neckline` (a gold V, a pendant with `gem`), `bell_sleeves`, `girdle`, `cuirass` (leaf scales and a gold leaf), `armour` (leaf-plate pauldrons), `tunic` (leaf-cut skirts below the belt), `leaf_cloak` (with `cloak`: the long leaf-hemmed cloak and mantle; a bow and quiver then sit over it), `shawl`, `leaf_hood` (pointed, the ears out through it), `cowl` (a hood down), `bracers`, `tall_boots` (to the knee, a pointed cuff), `sash`, `bow` (`True` slung on the back, `'hand'` held), `quiver`, `lute`, `lantern_pole`, `basket`, `satchel`, `leaf_apron` (+ `leaf2`), `flower_crown` (+ `flower`, `flower2`), `bard_cap` (+ `feather`), `braid` (`True` down the back, `'side'` over the right shoulder, `'twin'` one over each), `braid_tie`, `temple_braids`, `braid_crown`, `young` (child-sized, the head a little bigger); and from before `long_hair: 'flowing'`, `ponytail`, `gown`, `trim`, `belt`, `sword`, `eye`, `shoes`, skin `skin0`. Most take their colours as a tone tuple under their own name; defaults for the things (staff, lantern glow, wicker, wool, flowers, herbs, lute) are in `FIXED`.

## Character sprite sheets

Every walking character (the pilot now, NPCs next) uses this format:

| Spec | Value |
|---|---|
| Frame size | 32 × 48 native px (1 tile wide, 1.5 tall), feet 3 px above the bottom |
| Rows (direction order) | down, right, up, left |
| Columns | idle 0-3, then walk 0-7 — 12 × 4 = 384 × 192 px per sheet |
| Idle | ~340 ms per frame, sequence 0-1-2-1 |
| Walk | frame chosen from distance walked (8 frames per 2.4 tiles), not time |

The pilot uses this path (see "Characters — the Blender pipeline" below); the procedural drawer in `js/character.js` is only a fallback. NPCs are still procedural 16-unit tiles and should move to the same pipeline next.

The pilot's **attack sheet** (`pilot-attack[-<style>]-<a|b>-<head>.png`, every suit × build × head like the others) is its own layout, written by `tools/bridge-art/attacks.py` to `assets/characters/attacks.json` (the game reads the column names from there — never hard-code them):

| Spec | Value |
|---|---|
| Frame size | 80 × 92 native px (room for lunges, spins and the roll), the floor point at (40, 60) |
| Rows | down, right, up, left |
| Columns (39) | each weapon class's key poses, then the roll: `blade-` ready jab cross cut coil lunge · `sword-` wind sweep left right spin1 spin2 spin3 · `chop-` raise fall through raise2 through2 · `pole-` draw thrust reach back long · `maul-` heft over slam quake rise · `cast-` raise burst after · `pick-` up swing strike · `roll-` dive tuck1 tuck2 tuck3 rise — 3120 × 368 px |
| Timing | not in the sheet: js/arms.js strings the poses into swings with milliseconds per pose (`CLASSES`) |

**Weapons in hand** (`assets/characters/weapons/<id>.png` + `<id>.json`, `tools/bridge-art/weapon_sprites.py`): each weapon rendered in the pilot's hand for every pose of its class and every idle/walk frame (carrying it), for builds A and B, with the body held out (so whatever the hand, arm or body hides is already cut away), cropped and packed into one atlas. The JSON gives, per build → direction → frame name (`sword-sweep`, `walk3`, `idle0`…), `[x, y, w, h, dx, dy]` (dx, dy: the cropped frame's corner from the pilot's floor point), and `anchors`: the hand and the weapon's tip in each frame (px from the floor point) and whether the hand is in front of the body — the swing trails are drawn through the tip's path. `character.js drawFighter` puts the pilot frame and the weapon frame together and draws them as one sprite (depth-tested with `flat`: what's below the foot line lies on the floor in front, so a maul slammed down or a pike thrust toward you isn't hidden as if underground).

## Engine integration

### Adding a new PNG building / prop
1. Drop the painted PNG in `apps/bridge/assets/buildings/{name}.png` (or `props/`, `creatures/`).
2. Add it to `MANIFEST` in `tools/bridge-art/native_sprites.py` with its footprint width in tiles (and `True` for the matte flood if it has a dark background instead of white).
3. Run `python3 tools/bridge-art/native_sprites.py {name}` — it writes `assets/native/{name}.png` at exactly 32 px per tile (aspect kept, halo stripped, palette-aware downscale) and updates `assets/native/manifest.js`.
4. Draw it from a tile drawer with `BridgeSprites.draw(ctx, x, y, ts, '{name}', tilesW, tilesH, anchorOffsetX)` (or the world's `drawBuildingSprite`). It sits on the anchor tile's bottom edge, centred on the footprint, over a soft contact shadow.
5. Position overlays (smoke, window glow, spray) as fractions of `BridgeSprites.area(...)` — the rect the art actually occupies — never of the raw footprint.
6. In the world JSON, put the drawer's tile id on the anchor (bottom-centre) tile, set the footprint tiles to `0` (void) with `collisions = 1`.

### Characters — the Blender pipeline
Every character (the pilot, NPCs) is a cel-shaded 3D model rendered straight to 32 × 48 native frames by `tools/bridge-art/characters.py`, using the same 45° camera and tile units as the pre-rendered scenes (so a seated NPC sits at exactly stool height):

```
python3 -m venv BVENV && BVENV/bin/pip install bpy==5.2.2   # Python 3.13
BVENV/bin/python tools/bridge-art/characters.py /tmp/chars/pilot pilot
python3 tools/bridge-art/sprites_post.py pilot /tmp/chars/pilot
BVENV/bin/python tools/bridge-art/characters.py /tmp/chars/npc-bartender npc-bartender
python3 tools/bridge-art/sprites_post.py npc /tmp/chars/npc-bartender
```

- Shading happens in the shader: N·L against a fixed key light (high, upper-left), three flat tone bands per material, no lamps, one sample, no filtering, no dithering — every pixel is a palette colour.
- `sprites_post.py` draws line work where one body part overlaps another (from id + depth passes) and the outer outline, then packs one sheet per colour variant into `assets/characters/`.
- Sheet layout: rows down/right/up/left; columns idle 0-3, walk 0-7. Frames are 32 × 48 and the floor point under the character (feet, or seat for sitting poses) is pixel (16, 45).
- **Faces are painted, not modelled** (Stardew-style). The head has no eye or nose blocks: `characters.py` projects points on the head's sphere (`FACE_POINTS`: eyes, brows, mouth, cheeks, plus the chin — the head's lowest point on screen) into every frame, with how squarely each faces the camera, and writes them with the face's id colour and what to paint to `anchors.json` beside the frames. `sprites_post.py` paints the face onto the head's own pixels only, so hats, fringes, beards, masks and shades still cover it. The rows hang off the chin (from the eyes to the chin is under 4 px, so each point rounded on its own would put the mouth off the chin in some frames): eyes 2 × 3 with a white catch-light top left, brows a row above them in the hair's deep tone, blush beside the eyes, a smile (or a straight mouth) on the chin row. Side-on it's one dark 1 × 2 eye at most; from behind, nothing. **idle3 is idle1 with the eyes shut** — a blink; character.js, multi.js and crowd.js show it one beat in twelve (otherwise idle runs 0-1-2-1).
- Face options per NPC (`NPCS`): `smile` (default on; off for the stern ones), `blush`, `brows` (a colour; default the hair's deep tone), `eye` (tones: an iris — Beckolas's blue). `shades` and `visor_band` hide the eyes, `mask` the mouth; the pilot in the helmet has no face.
- On **key-colour sheets** (the pilot's, the crowd's) the eyes, mouth and catch-light are no key colour, so look.js and crowd.js leave them alone; the brows are the hair key's deep tone, so they recolour with the hair; blush on a crowd sheet is `XBLUSH`, which crowd.js swaps for that person's skin flushed pink. A new face colour must not be a key colour or a line-work copy of one.
- The walk swings the arms 39° and bobs the body ±0.675 units (same frames and timing as before). The weapons' carry frames follow the walk, so changing it means re-rendering them too (`WEAPONS=only` in pilots.sh).
- New NPCs are a `NPCS` entry in `characters.py` (skin, shirt, hair, hat, beard, apron, vest, pose — and the elves' options, see *Sylvara* under World palettes). A sheet renders in seconds, so look at it big before it goes in (frames scaled ×8) and again in the world at game scale. Dress options added for the Neon Royale, shared by anyone: `waistcoat` (closed, brass buttons, and a back — a white shirt's back reads as bare skin under warm light), `bib` (a shirt front down a jacket — a dinner suit is a dark `shirt` + `bib` + `bowtie`; a tuple colours it), `bowtie`, `coat` (a greatcoat's skirts, two rows of buttons, gold epaulettes), `sequin` (glints over a dress), `stole` (fur round the shoulders), `tray` (a server's tray of drinks at the shoulder), `shades`, `earpiece`, `gloves` (True white, or a tuple), `chrome_arm` (the left arm in steel), `pants` (a tone for the trousers: a white suit).
- **The pilot is customisable** (`js/look.js`, BridgeLook; localStorage `bridge_look`): build (A broad / B slight — `body_b()` reshapes any suit), hairstyle (`HAIRSTYLES`: short, long, ponytail, bun, curls, mohawk, shaved), a beard, the helmet on or off, skin tone and hair colour. Sheets are rendered per pose × suit × build × head (a head is `helmet`, `<hair>` or `<hair>-beard`) into `assets/characters/pilots/pilot[-sit|-ski|-climb|-attack][-<style>]-<a|b>-<head>.png` by `tools/bridge-art/pilots.sh` (all 70 runs; the 56 walking/seated/sport ones took ~45 min on two cores, the 14 attack runs and two weapon runs ~90 min, two at a time). Suit, skin and hair are rendered in **key colours** (`KEYS` in characters.py and look.js — keep the two in step) and look.js swaps them, and their line-work copies, as a sheet loads; so suit colours, skin tones and hair colours are free to add (a list entry in look.js, no re-render). A sheet key in character.js reads `[sit-|ski-|climb-|attack-][<style>-]<colour>@<look code>`. New pilots pick their look on the NEW PILOT screen; the wardrobe's *Change look* changes it later. With nothing saved, the helmet stays on.
- New pilot suits are a `build_<style>()` in `characters.py` (sharing `legs`, `arms` and `helmet` — which also builds the bare head and every hairstyle), added to the style map in the pilot branch and to `pilots.sh`; then an entry in `SUITS` in `js/gear.js`.
- **Fighting poses** (`pilot-attack[-<style>]-<a|b>-<head>`): `tools/bridge-art/attacks.py` holds the poses as plain numbers (per pose: the waist's lean and twist, each arm, each leg, where the weapon points — `aim` in the pilot's own frame — and how far along the haft the other hand reaches, `off`; a lunge, a spin, the roll's tumble), and `attack_rig.py` rigs the built suit for them (a waist pivot so the shoulders turn over planted feet, a grip in each hand) and turns the wrist to aim the weapon. `characters.py … pilot-weapons-<a|b>` builds every weapon in `weapon_sprites.py WEAPONS` in the grip and renders each one through its class's poses and the walk frames (the body as a holdout; the id pass marks the body white so the packer knows where it covers the weapon). `pilots.sh` does it all: `MODES="attack" WEAPONS=1 tools/bridge-art/pilots.sh BVENV/bin/python 2` (14 attack runs, one per suit × build doing all 15 heads, ~10 min each under load at two at a time, plus two weapon runs of ~4 min; packing runs on `PACK`, default `python3`, which needs numpy and Pillow — the Blender venv has neither). `MODES` picks the poses (`stand sit ski climb attack`), `WEAPONS=only` renders just the weapons (after adding one). Quick looks: `ATTACK_WEAPON=cutlass BVENV/bin/python tools/bridge-art/characters.py OUT pilot-attack-a short` renders the sheet with a weapon in hand as part of the pilot. Changing a pose means re-rendering every attack sheet (and the weapons); `python3 tools/bridge-art/attacks.py` rewrites attacks.json when columns change.

### Portraits (the faces on the talk card)
When you talk to someone their face is beside what they say: a portrait, Stardew-style — head and shoulders from the front, drawn. (It used to be cropped out of the walking sheet, which is drawn from 45° above: mostly the top of a head, or a hat. Beck: "it would be nice if it made a face for them for that picture. Kind of like Stardew." Then the Blender models seen from the front, which he found "too just blob like … more like Stardew Valley… a little more drawn"; from three drawn styles he picked Stardew's proportions with the in-between round's slightly bigger eyes, October 2026.)
- **Drawn** by `tools/bridge-art/portraits.py` — no Blender: everything about a person comes from characters.py's tables (`NPCS`, `EXTRAS`, `KEYS`, `XKEYS`, `FIXED`), read as data. A 64 × 64 frame, the way a pixel artist draws a portrait: a face with cheekbones, a jaw and a chin (a bald head a round dome), ears (an elf's long ones), a neck, sloped shoulders and sleeves; hair in locks with strands and a band of shine (a swept fringe, long hair falling past the shoulders, a bun pulled back with a centre part, a ponytail, a cloud of curls, a mohawk, braids over a shoulder, round the crown or at the temples); a beard, a mustache. Every shape is a signed-distance shape in frame units, lit by how it would bulge (`shade`: a rounded rim, flat inside) from the upper left in three flat bands, shadows leaning cool and highlights warm; clothes take the light of the body under them (`form`), so a vest is a panel on a chest, not a cushion; casters shift their shadow down and right (a fringe on the forehead, a brim, the chin on the neck); a near-black line round the figure, a softer one wherever one thing lies over another. Then the face, from pixel templates (`FACES`) stamped only on the face's own pixels, so a fringe, a hat, shades or a mask cover what they cover: eyes with a lid, a white, an iris with its catch-light (lashes on the women), brows, a nose's shadow and nostril, a mouth (in a beard, a slit over a lip), blush, a line under the eyes when the hair's grey.
- **What they wear** comes from their flags: shirts with a collar, vests and waistcoats (buttons), aprons, a greatcoat with epaulettes, a dinner suit's shirt front and lapels, bow ties, gowns with a scoop neckline and trim, a gold V and pendant, sequins, a fur stole, a shawl or a leaf mantle with a clasp, leaf pauldrons and a breastplate, an elf's tunic over a shirt, straps across the chest (quiver, bow, satchel), a steel arm; and on the head a chef's hat, caps and visor caps, beanies, straw and witch's hats, hoods (a leaf hood with the ears through it), a hood fallen back, circlets and a crown, a beret with a feather, flower crowns, goggles up, shades, a mask, a visor band, an earpiece. A new flag on a model wants a line here too (`npc_body`, `hats`).
- **Expressions**: each file is a strip of five frames — neutral, happy, surprised, sad, blink. Neutral is their temperament (`MOODS` in portraits.py: most smile; some are stern, flat or smirk). The card blinks now and then; in free talk a reply's mood (or which way their feeling moved) picks the face (`expressionOf`, js/interactions.js); signing someone on makes them happy; the Queen-Regent smiles when the text says she does.
- **Where they live**: `assets/portraits/<sheet>.png` (every NPC in its own colours, named as its sheet; its iris from its `eye`, else picked by its name — Queen Alyssara's are brown), `assets/portraits/crowd/<body>.png` (the crowd's bodies in their key colours, XKEYS), `assets/portraits/pilots/[<style>-]<a|b>-<head>.png` (every suit, build and head in the pilot's key colours, KEYS: the people you hire, captains, another pilot you walk up to; each suit drawn as itself — the flight suit's pads and chest lights, the coat's lapels and epaulettes, plate, the thermal's quilting and fur ruff, the runner's high collar and piping, the robe, the ranger's mantle — and the helmet's visor is glass: the face shows through it, a pale rim and a glint). Key-coloured ones use nothing but each key's three tones and their line-work copies (`line_of`, as js/look.js `lineOf`), two keys of their own — the iris and the blush (`IRIS_KEY`, `BLUSH_KEY`), swapped per person by js/look.js `portraitKeys` (keep them in step with portraits.py) — and colours that are never swapped (the eyes, the mouth, brass, steel, glass, fur).
- **A new NPC**: its `NPCS` entry in characters.py, then `python3 tools/bridge-art/portraits.py npc-<name>` (a fraction of a second). Its temperament is a line in `MOODS` (smile is the default), `ROSY` for ruddy cheeks. `npcs`, `crowd`, `pilots` or `all` do the sets (`all`: 358 files, about a minute); `--look FILE` writes a ×4 contact sheet of what it made — look at it before it goes in, and at a key-coloured one recoloured. A new crowd body, suit or hairstyle: re-run its set.
- **How the game picks one**: `BridgeLook.portrait(canvas, path, opts)` (js/look.js) draws it — whole on a 64 canvas (or a whole multiple of it), at half size (each pixel the average of four) on a smaller one — or, with no file, falls back to the old crop of the sheet. The talk card (`drawPortrait`: the person's `sheet` in people.json, else the scene actor on the interaction's tile), any chooser given a `face` (a passer-by, js/crowd.js; the Queen-Regent, js/crown.js; another pilot, js/multi.js via `BridgeLook.pilotFace`), `BridgeLook.face` (staff and captains: the ledger, the staff board, business and harbour lists, 24 × 24 at half size) and `BridgeLook.npcFace` (a crew member's own). On the card it's 64 px shown ×2 on a desktop (`.wd-portrait`: 128 px inside a 2 px ring each dialog theme draws) and ×1 on a phone. Test: `world/portraits.js`.

### Pre-rendered Blender scenes (the target for every world)
Rooms and areas are being rebuilt as Blender scenes. A scene is a script in `tools/bridge-art/scenes/` that places parts from an asset kit on the world's own tile grid, renders it, and rewrites the world JSON to match what was built (`scenekit.apply_world`: collisions from the placed objects, interactions moved onto their objects, NPC actors, the `scene` reference). Kits: `assets_lumar.py` (Lumar rooms — floors, walls, furniture, shop fittings, fireplaces, lamps), `assets_estate.py` (the grand rooms: chevron parquet, cabochon marble and hex mosaic, Persian rugs with real borders and medallions, panelled walls under damask or stripes with cornices, sash windows with swagged drapes, gilt frames with portraits, marines and still lifes, marble chimneypieces, crystal chandeliers, sconces and torchères, the grand staircase and gallery, floor-to-ceiling bookcases, seating that faces any way (`Local`), a table laid for dinner, a four-poster, a roll-top bath, statuary; and the Starling's rattan, palms, fans and bar), `assets_penthouse.py` (black marble, teak decking with holes for a pool, glass walls and balustrades, a back bar, a kitchen, a sectional, a platform bed, an egg bath, the plunge pool, loungers, the fire pit, planters, festoons, the tower's face), `assets_magic.py` (octagonal masonry rooms, angled windows, spell circles, candelabras, telescope…), `assets_cave.py` (rock faces, cave floor, lava, the dragon, hoard).

Done so far — every world: the tavern, inn, bakery, smithy, apothecary, the sorceress's tower, the dragon's cave, the pilot's quarters, the bridge and the captain's office (`assets_ship.py`), the six Arcadia interiors (`assets_cyber.py` — neon stroke-font signs, arcade cabinets with per-game screen art, shop/bar/clinic/pawn/repair fittings), Enigma Station and the Singularity (`assets_station.py`), and the outdoor worlds (`assets_town.py` — houses with shingled gable roofs and window boxes, cobbles, the fountain, bell tower, market stalls, trees, benches, bunting, lantern posts, running-bond flagstones, the broadside galleon and rowboats; `assets_cyber.py` storefronts, street lamps, puddles and planters for the Arcadia District): Lumar Harbour, the Market Square, the four islands, the Arcadia District. The Neon Royale (the casino) has its own kit, `assets_casino.py` (see *The Neon Royale* below).

```
BVENV/bin/python tools/bridge-art/scenes/lumar_tavern.py /tmp/tavern
python3 tools/bridge-art/scene_post.py /tmp/tavern apps/bridge/assets/scenes/lumar-tavern
```

- **Camera:** orthographic, 45° elevation; the world is stretched along rows by 1/sin 45° so every floor tile lands on an exact 32 × 32 px cell — the render lines up with the game grid.
- **Light is real, finished as toys:** Cycles renders lamps, fire, sun and moonlight with cast shadows. `scene_post.py` finishes it in the toy style (`STYLE = 'toy'`, the default since October 2026): the light's luminance through the tone curve into six flat bands (`TOY_BANDS`), part of the light's colour kept (warm firelight stays warm), shadows leaning cool and the brightest light warm (`TOY_COOL`), and a strong line (`TOY_LINE`, a pixel darkened to ~40%) on a thing wherever what's next to it lies behind it or nothing does — a softer one where two things meet without a step — but never on the ground (`GROUND_WORDS`: paths, turf, paving, floors, rugs, water — by the words in the part's name, from render.json `ids`) and on a roof only at its real edges (`ROOF_WORDS`). Name parts so this reads: a floor is `floor_*`, a path `path`, a roof `roof_*`. `SCENE_STYLE=dither` re-posts a render in the older look (many dithered steps, soft lines).
- **Firelight flickers:** each scene renders two light states (`full`, `dim`); the engine cross-fades them.
- **Depth, not layers:** `depth.png` stores per-pixel depth; `js/scene.js` depth-tests every character against it (a character is an upright billboard at its foot row), so walking behind tables, counters and stools is always right — no hand-sorted layers. People walking about (you and the other pilots: `drawOccluded(…, 'auto')`) show through as a faded copy of themselves once more than half of them is hidden — behind a lodge's long roof or a tall cabinet you'd otherwise vanish; behind a counter your legs just go (Beck saw people "being clipped"). Posed sports (ski, climb) use `xray: true`, a pale silhouette. **Someone sitting** isn't upright at the floor point under their seat: facing you or side-on their lap reaches forward over it, and tested there the seat's own front half was drawn across their waist (Beck: "this guy is clipping through the bench"). So seated figures — you, other pilots, passers-by, scene actors with `pose: "sit"` — are drawn on the seat but depth-tested and sorted from its front edge, `BridgeScene.seatedRow(row, facing)`: a quarter tile nearer (every bench, chair and stool is behind that; a table or desk in front still hides the lap), a tenth facing away (a chair's back between you and them still hides them). `tools/tests/world/seats.js` checks every seat in actions.json and every seated actor.
- **Characters pick up the room's light** from a per-tile light grid the render bakes (`tileLight` in `scene.json`).
- **Walkable height** (`scene.heights` — not `scene.lift`, which is Hoarfell's chairlift cables — in the world JSON's scene block, an array of rows: the ground's height in tiles of z at every tile's centre; Lanternfall's terraces, Rosewell's pools, the bathhouse's bath): wherever the ground stands up, everyone standing there — you, other pilots, walkers, the crowd, actors, your pet, an event's cast — is drawn raised by z·cos 45°, their feet on the terrace (`BridgeScene.drawOccluded` does it for all of them, the height blended between tile centres, so a stair is a ramp). The foot row stays the true row, which is what keeps the depth test right: a pixel h tiles above raised feet is h + z up and the depth formula already gives it that depth, so a terrace's own surface never hides you and the house in front of it still does. Below zero sinks you: a pool whose tiles are at its bed's height takes you in to the waist, the water's surface hiding the rest. The camera, the prompt pill and the highlight at the foot follow (`BridgeScene.liftAt(sc, x, r)` in tiles, `liftPx(sc, x, footRow)` in native px); the live light's tint reads the light up there. Build it from the same height function the scene's land is built from (`scenes/lumar_cliff.py` `H`, written as `scene_extra={'heights': …}` to `apply_world`); nothing changes for a world without it. Not yet: the baked `tileLight` probe is a flat plane (a raised figure takes its light from the live passes once they've loaded), event bubbles and name tags float at ground level.
- NPCs are `actors` in the world JSON's `scene` block (sheet, tile, facing, `pose: "sit"`), drawn with idle animation.
- **Scenekit primitives** (all in tile units — x, row, z): `box`, `cyl`, `cyl_x`, `cyl_y`, `sphere`, `cone`, plus oriented ones — `bar` (a box between two floor points, for angled walls and rune strokes), `tube` (cylinder/cone between two 3D points: limbs, telescopes, horns), `blob` (rotated ellipsoid), `rock` (faceted boulder) `poly` (flat surface: wing membranes, sails), `loft` (skin through cross-section rings: hulls, curved shells) and `boxes` (many boxes as one part — a tower's thousand lit windows). `assets_town` adds `annulus` (ring walls: basins, rims), `pyramid` (square roofs lined up with the walls — a 4-sided cone sits at 45°) and `lantern` (open iron frame round lit glass — never enclose an emissive part in a solid box). Meshes are built with bmesh, so a scene of a thousand-plus parts builds in seconds.
- **Glows add on top of the lit colour.** The glow you see is the emission colour × its strength (capped at 1); a strength above 1 only casts more light on the room. Give a glowing part a dark albedo and a saturated colour; bright albedo + strong emission clips to white. For a faint glow (puddles, coins under water) use a low strength.
- Mark tiles an object stands on with `k.solid(...)`, including tiles NPC actors stand on — `apply_world` only knows what you mark. Also block walkable tiles where a tall object in front would hide the player almost completely (behind cabinets, vending machines, the strip behind a row of houses). Along a side no longer than a tile, `k.solid` blocks only the cells the footprint covers at least 0.3 of (always at least the one under its centre): a barrel or a tree trunk blocks one tile, not two or four. Thin posts (lamps, flagpoles) use `k.solid_post(x, r)`: they block only the tiles where your feet would be on the pole (you stand at the bottom of your tile), so a post on a tile line is walked past on either side. Don't pad small props with a bigger `k.solid` of your own — what looks walkable should be — and keep a street's free row clear of posts, stalls and people.
- **Don't overlap coplanar boxes** (e.g. per-tile ground slabs that overhang each other): they catch each other's shadows and the tile grid shows as dark seams. Build one box per run of cells, or butt them exactly.
- **Coasts and water (Lumar):** land meets the sea in the render, not at a tile edge. `assets_coast.coast(k, walkable_cells, palette=…)` grows a heightfield round the walkable ground with a wandering edge (headlands and coves a tile in or out): a grass or sand lip, rock banks or beaches down to the waterline at `WZ` (z = −0.34; the ground is z = 0), a shallow shelf with kelp and glowing spore clumps, the seabed falling to the deep, rocks standing in the surf. Above the waterline on a beach the sand goes damp in a wandering band where the waves wash (`damp_sand`), wetter and a little shiny just at the water (`wet_sand`); along the top of it lies the wrack line — strands of weed, a shell, now and then a stick of driftwood — on its own dice, so the scene's dice place everything after the coast where they always did. It returns a `Terrain` (`.z(x, r)`) to set things on the banks. Ships ride in it: build them with `waterline=False` and lower them with `assets_coast.sink(k, since, WZ)` so the keel shows through the shallows. Setting `k.water_z` renders a height pass; `scene_post` writes `shore.png` (R: px from the waterline, G: depth under the surface, B: empty) and `scene.json` gets `water`.
- **The sea in the engine** (`js/sea.js`), in the toy finish (Look Book 2's water): each sea's colours (night, dusk and day palettes blended by the sky) in a few flat bands — wide swells of deeper and lighter water and short crisp wavelets (a light dash over a dark one) on seamless maps worked out once (`waveMap`: 512 × 256, 384 × 192, 320 × 160, an index a pixel) and coloured when the light changes (`colours`, keyed); no noise, no dithering. A storm or gale fades whitecaps in (a third map, in two steps), rain and storms grey it and ring it where the drops come down (`rainRings`: Look Book 2's rain rings, in cells of the world, on the open sea and an island's water; `BridgeSea.waterAt` tells js/weather.js where the water is, so it lands no splash there), fog washes it out; golden hour warms it. Over it the light of the hour (`skyLight`, points on a world-anchored grid so nothing shimmers as the camera moves): the sun's sparkle by day, a glitter path toward the low sun at golden hour (west in the evening), the moon road and mirrored stars on clear nights. The light comes from the scene's sky (`BridgeScene.skyOf`), or its live light's hour once composed (`BridgeLit.light`, so `_force` moves the sea too), and `BridgeWeather.here` (`BridgeSea._light(palette, worldId)` shows it). `BridgeSea.drawSea` is the open sea behind a scene and `BridgeSea.drawWater` (the Lumar `ground` hook, right after the render) lays the same moving water over everything below the surface: sheltered water — harbours, coves, along a quay — lying still and darker like Look Book 2's harbour (`shelter`: rays toward the land in eight directions on an 8-px grid at load, spread to full size with a wandering edge; `calmOver` covers the swell and its wavelets there with one flat body in Look Book 2's still-water colours — teal-grey by day, warm grey at golden hour, deep blue at night, a fifth of the sea's own kept — leaving the mirror, the sparkle and the moon road on it; a storm reaches in), its depth in four flat bands with wandering shelves (`SHELF`, opacity `BAND_A`, the shallows' pale tint `BAND_T`, the lip of each shelf a little lighter) so sand, rocks and kelp show through the shallows, solid in the deep where it meets the open sea without a seam; what stands above the waterline — banks, quays, piers, moored hulls, rocks — mirrored in it (`mirrorSegs` from shore.png: runs of columns with the same waterline, as far down as the thing is tall, `MAXR` 40 px; the picture is the live composition or the light state that shows most), darkened to the water's tone, fading in three steps and rippling a row at a time (made for the view and a margin, not the whole render); lit windows and lanterns (the scene's `glow` image) streaking down it after dark; light rippling on the shallow bed; foam in the toy style — a lap line at the waterline breathing a pixel in and out, surf lines rolling in (quicker and wider in a storm). Out sailing, js/sloop.js mirrors every boat in the water (`mirrored`: the hull's side, masts and sails upside down in rows nudged on the same wave, the stern lantern streaking down at night; broken up in rough weather). Composite the waves into the water mask with a pattern fill — `source-in` applies to the whole canvas on every draw, so tiled drawImage calls wipe each other.
- **Holes onto live art:** a part with albedo `'HOLDOUT'` renders transparent (viewports onto space). The world's `scene.sky` rect (screen tiles) tells the world script where to draw its animated sky; register it as the `behind` hook (below).
- **Foliage:** parts built inside `with k.foliage('leaf')` (one tree or bush's leaves — they turn with the seasons) or `k.foliage('needle')` (an evergreen's) are marked by a leaf pass (`leaf.png`, one sample): `scene_post.py` writes `leaves.png` (R a leaf, B a needle, G which tree, `8 + 16 × (tree mod 15)`) beside the render and each variant layer, and `scene.json` gets `leaves: 1` (a layer `leaves: 1`). js/scene.js loads it and hands it, composited with the layers, to the scene's grade (below). The tree builders in `assets_town.py`: `leafy_crown` (a dark core and dozens of small clumps over it, lighter toward the key light, sprigs past the edge — leaves, where one smooth ball read as a lump), `tree` (a tapering trunk with a root flare and branches under a crown of three or four lobes; returns the lobes, for fruit — `assets_farm.apple_tree` hangs its apples on them), `pine_tiers` / `pine` (tiers fringed with drooping sprays, a ragged outline; `snow` lays snow on each tier and its sprays — `assets_snow.snow_pine`; `assets_isle.red_pine` is the same in Crimson's colours), `bush` (a small leafy crown). Sylvara's birches and giant canopies are tagged too. The same pass marks **the grass** (R 64: the turf, the meadow's blades and flowers, an island's grass lip) and **still water** (B 64: ponds, streams, fountain basins) by part name (`Kit.GRASS_PARTS`, `WATER_PARTS`, less a `ground_` / `coast_` prefix) or a part's own `nature` (assets_coast tags its grass lip) — for the seasons on the ground and js/nature.js. Ground that runs on for many tiles (`Kit.GROUND`: an island's turf, paths, banks and beaches, a lawn) takes its grain in tile units (object coordinates): over an object's own extent the noise stretched into wandering contour lines across a whole island — squiggles where the farm read as an even texture.
- **Grass and ground** (Beck: "stardew has a lot more detail in the grass… want it to be super pretty", then "more realistic… it is too much"): `W.grass` lays the turf and, once the scene is laid out (`k.later`, run at the start of `render`), `W.meadow` grows the grass over it wherever the turf is still the top surface (`k.top(x, r)`: a ray straight down, returning the part name and height — nothing grows through paths, floors or under houses). The grass is a carpet of small tufts (`k.blades`: thousands of thin triangles as a few meshes, one per tone): greens only a little apart (the shadowed ones, the turf's own, sunlit ones with a few bright tips), their mix drifting lighter and darker across the meadow in slow patches (`_vnoise`), a tall clump now and then, flowers in clumps of one kind (`FLOWERS`), clover patches, the odd pebble. The blades share the turf's part (`'grass'`), so `scene_post.py` draws no line work round each one — a texture, not scribbles. Turf creeps over the edges of paths and fields (`ragged_edges`, from `assets_farm.dirt_path` / `earth`: bumps of turf and tufts leaning out, wherever turf lies just beyond). Paths are sandy dirt (`assets_farm.DIRT`) speckled light and dark with small stones (a stretched noise grain read as wood). Islands get the same through `assets_isle.tufts` (`flowers=` lower on Midnight and Crimson). **Drawn over it** (Beck, next: "more actual grasses and drawn textures… not random blobs… subtle little grass… down to that small level"): `scene_post.grass_detail` stamps small hand-drawn marks into the albedo over the grass the leaf pass marks, before it's lit (so every state shows them) — tufts with a shadow at their foot, a pair of blades, one blade, a shadowed tuft, clover, a daisy or buttercup, a pebble (`GRASS_MARKS`, drawn a pixel at a time: h/l lighter than the grass round them, m/d/s darker, flower and pebble colours absolute), one to a 12 × 9 px cell, thicker in some stretches; the grass between them is calmed 75% toward its local colour, so the ground reads as a drawn texture, not noise. The meadow's slow light and dark drifts are kept faint (strong ones read as blobs). The dusk light is a warm gold sun under a blue-grey sky (`_cycle_states`: a violet sky turned every tan surface pink).
- **Roofs and house fronts** (Beck: "the roof is just like kind of a square with like some lines on it… we could do a little bit better… more detail on things"): `assets_town._gable_roof` lays a dark deck under courses of separate shingles on both slopes (`k.quads`, a few tones and the odd weathered one), each course lapping the one below with its butt standing proud and a dark line where it shadows the course under it, a pixel's joint between shingles showing the deck; half-round ridge tiles, bargeboards up the gable ends, a gutter along the eave with a downpipe, moss and lichen thicker toward the eaves and on the back slope. Snow (`ROOF_SNOW`) lies as a smooth blanket with soft drifts and a lumpy cornice. `chimney_stack` builds brick courses over mortar with a cap and clay pots. `dress_front` puts ivy up a stretch of wall clear of the door and windows (foliage leaves, so it turns red in autumn) and potted plants by the door. Every `W.house`, the barn, the forge and the chalets use them. Each house has its own character (Beck: "more variation in character"): a plaster tint, timber and shutter colours, a shed dormer in the front slope of a wide house (`dormer`), a hood over the door (`door_hood`), a weathervane now and then — by the scene's dice. The barn's sliding doors are framed all round in white with flat X braces (round tubes shaded grey on one side), on a track with hangers and handles; a louvred cupola with a weathervane on its ridge. The foot of every house is built too (Beck's "walls and clutter" next): the footing's face laid in two courses of dressed stone blocks, each its own tone with a pixel of dark joint, the odd one mossy (`footing_face`); plaster walls weathered as the wall's own colour, so no line work rings it — rising damp along the foot above the sill rail, rain streaks under the sills, now and then a patch of fallen plaster with brick behind it, clear of the posts and windows (`weather_wall`); and small life inside its own frontage (`wall_foot`): a water butt under the downpipe, which now runs down the wall's corner, and a barrel, slatted crates or a broom at the other end — each placed only once the scene is laid out (`k.later`) and only where rays straight down find nothing but ground (`_clear_round`), so they never go through a bench or a stall a scene stood there. A house's dressing rolls its own dice (seeded by where it stands), so the scene's dice — and everything else they place — stay as they were. **The squares' paving** has small life of its own (`street_clutter(k, area, seed, straw=, chalk=)`, Beck's "street clutter in the open squares"): iron drains with a damp stain running to them, weeds and a dandelion in the joints, a cracked flag and its chips, a stone sunk with water standing in it, a fallen leaf or a feather, straw and a dropped apple round the stalls, chalk hopscotch where children play (at the nearest bare paving to the spot asked for: a stall, a post or a planter on it moves it along, `chalk`) — all flat, so nothing needs walking round, placed once the scene is laid out (`k.later`) only where paving (`PAVING`) is what's on top, on its own dice; the specks take the paving's own part name, so no line work rings them (a separate part read as a black dot). In Crownhaven's grand square, Brambleford's, Sapphire's market and the Market Square. Lamp posts (`lantern_post`) cast a pool of warm light on the ground under them at night (a second, low light, 1.6× the lantern's — a lamp up a post alone lit the ground no more than Lumar's moon does, so the pool was lost in the shading bands). Chimneys (`chimney_stack`) stand 0.8 over the ridge so their brickwork shows: bricks you can count on every face you see, a corbelled band, soot near the top, a slim cap, one to three clay pots with dark mouths, lead flashing. **The coast's sand line** follows a blurred mask of the land (the walkable cells are whole tiles; a sand line by distance from them stepped like a staircase), and the shallows' weed edges are jittered.
- **The detail standard** (Beck: "we need to like go around improving our renderings… going forward we do a better job of making sure these renderings are detailed and look realistic"): before a render ships, look at it at 2× and check — nothing reads as a plain box or a flat panel (roofs have courses, walls have boards, stone or timber, chimneys have bricks and pots); nothing big is one flat colour or a stretched blob of noise (large surfaces take their grain in tile units, or are built of pieces); edges between materials aren't tile staircases; white trim stays white (flat geometry, not round tubes that shade); repeated things vary (houses, trees, flowers); there's small life at the human scale (pots, ivy, crates, signs); and a test render of any new kit piece is looked at alone before it goes into scenes.
- **Swappable decor:** parts built inside `with k.variant(slot, key):` are left out of the base render and rendered alone in the room's light; `scene_post.py` writes them to `layers/<slot>/<key>/`. The world script calls `BridgeScene.setLayers(sceneId, {slot: key|null})` and the engine composites the chosen variants into the scene image and depth (the quarters' catalog decor and trophies work this way).
- **Live things in a scene world** register `BridgeWorld.registerSceneHooks(tileset, {behind, ground, items, over, post})`: `behind` draws before the render (shows through holes), `ground` right after it, under everyone (the Lumar water, the dock's containment field), `items` returns `{footRow, draw}` entries sorted with the characters — draw them with `BridgeScene.drawOccluded(...)` so furniture hides them correctly (the drone, the hologram, the Singularity's echoes) — `over` draws on top (steam, rain), and `post(ctx, buf, w, h, offX, offY, ts, now)` reworks the finished frame (the Singularity's gravitational warp, `js/horizon.js`). Hooks are per tileset, so a hook that only some of its worlds use keys off a block in the world JSON (`dock`, `horizon`, `skyline`) and does nothing without it. Hooks registered for `'*'` (`behind`, `ground`, `items`, `over`) run in every scene world after the tileset's own — the furniture in homes uses them. Registering again for the same tileset (or `'*'`) adds to what's there — each part runs in the order registered (magic, homes and the expedition all hook in).
- **Cache-busting:** `scene_post.py` stamps `scene.json` with `rev` (a hash of its images); the engine fetches `scene.json` fresh and loads its images with `?v=rev`, and cutscene sprites carry a per-entry `rev` in `cine.json`. A re-rendered scene can never pair new collisions (world JSON) with a cached old image — always re-run `scene_post.py`, never copy PNGs by hand.
- **Images are lossless WebP** (the other session measured a scene at 16.6 MB as PNG, 9 MB as WebP): `scene_post.py` writes every image of a scene — the states, depth, leaves, shore and the layers' — as WebP, lossless and `exact` (every pixel as written, transparent ones too: depth and the leaf pass are data), and puts `"ext": "webp"` in `scene.json`. Anything that loads a scene's images reads `meta.ext` (absent means PNG): `js/scene.js` (`sc.ext`), the chart, the map, the sloop and voyage, treasure maps, home furniture, the mine's rocks, the deck previews, the shore in js/sea.js. `python3 tools/bridge-art/to_webp.py [ids]` converted the older scenes (each image read back and checked against its PNG before the PNG goes); cutscene sprites (`cine_post.py`) stay PNG. **The glow image** (`glow.<ext>`, scene.json `glow: 1`, a layer's `glow: 1`): what gives off light, alone — the emission pass at its strongest state's strength (a lamp by night), clear everywhere else — so the GPU bloom (js/gpu.js, the other session's) blooms real lamps, windows and neon and never a bright piano key.
- **`scene.backdrop: true`** draws the world's own registered background (space, the void, the Lumar sea, Arcadia's skyline) wherever the render is empty — stations floating in space, islands in the sea. Such a world's camera follows the player (even with `fixedCamera`) and may show the whole render, margins included; a world JSON `"view": [x0, y0, x1, y1]` (tiles) narrows that, e.g. to keep the harbour framed. Never leave a render cut square where land meets it: a wider screen shows the cut, and the sloop draws every island whole from the sea. Diggen's Point (`scenes/lumar_harbor.py`) builds the whole headland in its render — cliffs running on past the map as one heightfield whose foot wanders off the tile grid, the town's roofs and bell tower over the brow, a wandering coast all round into deep water at the render's edge — so a wide screen just shows the point curving away into the sea.
- **Signs with real typefaces** (`assets_signs.py`, fonts in `tools/bridge-art/fonts/`, all OFL): `neon` (tubes tracing the letters' outlines), `letters` (extruded channel letters, lit or painted), `lightbox` (glowing box, dark lettering), `blade` (a vertical sign on brackets, one glyph a row) and a few neon icons. Pick a face per shop so every sign has its own character, and keep cap heights ≥ 0.5 tiles or the letters mush at 32 px a tile. `assets_dock.floor_text` paints stencil lettering flat on a deck.
- **The District kit** (`assets_district.py`): `frontage` builds a storefront by floors (cladding, window rows, balconies, fire escapes, AC units, side signs, posters, graffiti) and bespoke one-offs give each shop its own face — the arcade's canopy and portholes, the noodle bar's timber front, Lucky 7's bulb sign, the capsule hotel's pod wall, the club's monolith and velvet rope.
- **Arcadia's streets, rebuilt** (October 2026, to Rainline Street's standard — `assets_arcadia_rich.py`, used by the street, the Backs, the Skywalk and Skyport 7): the ground in pieces (`paving`: half-tile slabs each their own tone, cracked, sunk, stained; `kerb` with drains and a painted line; `road`: the hover lane sunk 0.06 below the kerbs, patched, cracked, oil-stained, dashed, with glowing studs; `crosswalk`, `tactile`, `island`, `channel`; `deck_plates` for steel decks; `hazard_stripes`), and the small life: bikes, cones, bollards, news boxes, fruit crates, A-boards, a delivery robot, a hover taxi and van, cats, pigeons, a rat, flyers, pallets, gas bottles, a shopping trolley, planters with real crowns, pipes, meter boxes, wall signs, floodlights, a windsock. Things in the sunk lane take `z=` (puddles, grates, manholes). **Wet after the rain** (`k.wet = AR.WET`, `{word: strength}`): scenekit renders the parts with those words in their names as a perfect mirror, once of the flat colours (`wet.png`) and once of what glows (`wet_glow.png`); `scene_post.py` lays them over each light state (`WET_MIX`, `WET_DARK`, `WET_GLOW`), the neon smeared down the wet ground — every sign shows twice, once on the wall and once in the road. Only scenes that set `k.wet` get it.
- **Arcadia's skyline** (`tools/bridge-art/skyline.py` + `assets_city.py` → `assets/backdrops/arcadia/`, drawn by `js/skyline.js`): three Blender layers at the scenes' 45° — `far` mega-towers sinking into smog, `mid` towers with blank holo-ad screens (CineKit anchors `ad<N>_a/_b` mark their corners) and `low`, the city under the walkway's railing — scrolled at different speeds, with a moon in smog, lanes of flying traffic, searchlights, animated holo-ads and rain (`BridgeSkyline.rain`, the arcadia `over` hook, for worlds with a `"skyline"` block). Since October 2026 the towers are modelled (`assets_city.megatower`, Beck: "a lot cooler… more of the modeled view with more detail… really really pretty"): real silhouettes — setbacks, slabs, twin towers on a podium joined by sky bridges, drums, spires tapering to a needle, cantilevered stacks — with floor slabs standing proud and windows lit the way a city is (offices a floor at a time, flats a room at a time, dark floors, each building its own light; mullions, blinds), balconies and AC units, lifts running up the faces, neon bands and corner edges, brands in real letters on the crowns (`BRANDS`: HALCYON, KAIJU, NOVA, OMNI…), blades of glyphs, roofs crowded with tanks, dishes, masts, pads, cranes and billboards; `detail` thins it for the far layer, which is built in haze-light walls. `low` is `roofscape_rich` (parapets, stair housings with a lit door, tanks, vents, roof gardens, solar panels, skylights, roof signs, lit windows down the faces, streets with lane light, headlights and tail-lights, the shops' glow). All three go through the toy finish (`cine_post.py` → scene_post). In js/skyline.js the gap between the towers' feet and the drop is filled with the low layer again at half size, deep in the haze, and the haze over the drop is thinner so the city below shows.
- Ship scenes use `flicker.mode: "pulse"` — a slow breathing cross-fade between the light states for LED strips, instead of firelight flicker.

### Live light (js/litscene.js)
Look Book 2's relight, in the game: outdoors the picture is put together live for the planet's hour, season and weather instead of blended from three baked states.
- **Rendering it** (`tools/bridge-art/scenekit.py`): any scene with a day (`cycle=`, or `SCENE_CYCLE`, or `SCENE_LIT=1`; `SCENE_NOLIT=1` never) also renders, at `SCENE_LIT_SAMPLES` (64) samples: `normal.png` (world normal), `lit_sky.png` (a white sky dome alone), `lit_sun0…5.png` (six white suns through the day, `LIT_SUNS`: how far from sunrise to sunset and the light's direction — each alone, with its own shadows; the grass casts none there, glowing parts are lit like any surface) and `lit_lamps.png` (the scene's own lamps and glowing parts as its first light state has them, no sky); over still water `refl.png` / `refl_glow.png` (a perfect mirror of the flat colours, the sky keyed magenta; and what glows alone); and render.json `ids` (each part's id colour → its name) and `lit`. Every variant gets the same passes. About twice the render time of the baked states.
- **Packing it** (`scene_post.py lit_pack`): `lit_albedo` (the flat colours, the drawn grass in them), `lit_a` (R the sky, G/B suns 0-1), `lit_b` (suns 2-4), `lit_c` (R sun 5, G the normal's z, B flags: 1 leaf, 2 needle, 4 grass, 8 still water, 16 ground, 32 roof), `lit_d` (R which tree, G line strength), `lit_lamps` — light stored as sqrt(L / 4) × 255, 1 being open ground under the white sky — and `lit_refl` / `lit_reflglow`; a layer has the same in its own folder (`lit: 1`, `litLamps`). scene.json `lit`: `{ suns: [u…], lamps, base, refl, reflGlow }`. The baked states are still written (the map, the sloop, the chart, treasure maps and anything else that reads a state image keep working, and they show until the live picture is ready).
- **In the game** (`js/litscene.js`, BridgeLit, loaded with the scene in the background): the hour comes from `BridgeClock.sky` on a 24-hour face (sunrise at 6, sunset at 19.6, the night stretched over the rest) and picks Look Book 2's sky palette for it; the sun's light is the two nearest sun passes blended (strength from its height, warmer and redder low down), the sky's light in the sky's colour, at night the moon (the noon pass, faint and blue) and the lamps (rising through dusk) and what glows. Seasons from `BridgeFoliage.season` (Lumar and Verdance): grass fresh / straw / dormant, each leafy tree its own autumn colour (more of them turned as the season goes on), blossom, bare winter boughs; on Verdance in winter snow on whatever faces up (the normal's z) and ice creeping in from a pond's shore. Weather from `BridgeWeather.here`: rain wets the ground and dims the sun, cloud and fog flatten it, snowfall lays a light snow. Then the toy finish (four flat bands, cool shadows, warm lights, the lines). Still water: four depth bands with organic shelves (worked out once), the far bank mirrored and wobbling, Blender's mirror for what stands in it, light dashes, a glitter path at golden hour, stars on clear nights, the lap line on the near shore, lamps streaking down it after dark, the glints and the rings (a fish rising, the rain) — all as Look Book 2 drew them,
as many to the pixel of water as it had (js/nature.js leaves water in live light to it). Falling water (parts named
`waterfall`, `falls`, `cascade`, `weir`: flag 64 in `lit_c`) runs with light streaking down it and foam churning and rings
spreading where it lands; in a hard frost only a thread down the middle still runs. Still water's own glow (a well's)
stays out of the glow image: its light at night is the water's. On Hoarfell the snow is coloured by the hour as the Look
Book's tarn was (`hoarfell()` in litscene.js): lit or shade from the sun's share of the light against the best-lit snow
that hour, the hollows a step down from the sky pass, pink-gold where the sun lands at either end of the day and a cool
blue-grey in the shade, white and blue at noon, blue under the lamps at night; a trodden lane a touch darker, ice (pale
and bluish) leaned blue with its marks kept, and a warm lean where the sun lands on everything else. A composition takes a few ms a frame (`BUDGET`) whenever the light has moved on enough to show — about once a real minute — and fades in over the last (`FADE`). Characters are lit from the same passes at their feet (`tint`): in shadow under a tree, gold at sunset, lamplit at night.
- **Hooks:** js/scene.js calls `BridgeLit.load(sc)`, `.layers(sc, use, masks)` (decor and stand-ins carry their lit passes), `.frame(sc)` in drawBackground, `.water(…)` after it and `.tint(…)` in tintAt; a scene without `lit` draws as before. Previews and tests: `BridgeLit._force({ hour, season, weather })` (hour on the 24-hour face; season `spring|summer|autumn|winter`; weather `clear|cloud|rain|fog|snow`), `BridgeLit._stats(id)`, `BridgeLit.light(id)`.
- The open sea has its own reflections, glitter path, moon road and stars (js/sea.js, *The sea in the engine*). Not yet: firelight flicker in the live picture (its lamps burn steady; the baked `full` / `dim` cross-fade only shows without live light), a per-scene sun path (every scene's sun comes from the same side). The re-renders are the backlog (`_claude_docs/requests.md`, *The new look*).

### Making a new place (the house style)
Every new place, and every place rebuilt, is made this way (Beck: "make it a goal… all the new places that we would make are going to adopt this").
1. **Look at the target first:** `tools/bridge-art/dioramas/ref/*.png` (Look Book 2's places in the toy finish, by day, at golden hour and at night) and the scripts that built them (`tools/bridge-art/dioramas/`, its README). That's the bar for detail and composition; a finished place should sit beside them and look like it belongs.
2. **Shape the land.** A heightfield or terraces (`assets_isle.ground` / `ridge`, the dioramas' own), banks that slope into water, nothing a flat board. Water sits in the land: a pond is sunk into it with muddy, pebbly, reedy banks and an irregular outline — never a circle in a field (Beck: "a random circle in the middle of a field"). Name still water `pond_water` / `stream_water` / `basin_water` (`Kit.WATER_PARTS`) so the live light gives it Look Book water.
3. **Vegetation in layers:** turf with drawn grass (`W.grass` + `W.meadow`, `grass_detail`), tufts and flowers in clumps, shrubs and hedges, trees with real crowns (`leafy_crown`, `tree`, `pine`) inside `k.foliage(...)` so they turn with the seasons; evergreens where the place wants them.
4. **Buildings from the richer house kit** (`assets_house.py`, `HK.rich_house` — the arguments of `assets_town.house` — `HK.bay_shop`, `HK.street`, `HK.garden`, `HK.pump`, `HK.tub`, `HK.barrels`, `HK.crate_stack`; `HK.install()` swaps it in for `W.house` everywhere in a script): roofs by material (thatch, pantile, shingle; hipped now and then) with deep eaves and rafter ends, jettied upper floors, porches and door canopies, framed windows with shutters and flower boxes, plinths and quoins, ivy and roses.
5. **Small life at the human scale:** barrels, crates, tubs of flowers, a cat on the step, washing, signs, street clutter (`street_clutter`), the odd thing out of place.
6. **Light it for its world** (`cycle='day'` for a place under the sun, `'night'` for Lumar's moonlit towns — either way it gets live light) and lamps where the art shows a source; interiors keep their own light states (firelight `full` / `dim`) and get the toy finish.
7. **Name parts so the finish reads them** (the ground words, `roof_*`) and keep the detail standard below.
8. **Look at it at 2× in the game** at dawn, noon, golden hour and night, in each season, before it ships (`BridgeLit._force`), next to the reference images — `node tools/tests/world/vsref.js <world> <ref> [x0,y0,x1,y1] [x,y]` writes your place in the game beside the Look Book's at noon, golden hour and night — and run `node tools/tests/run.js world`.

**Reuse the Look Book's pieces.** The engine draws a Look Book place as the Look Book did (October 2026: Millpond Farm through the game beside its reference matched in light, shadow, water and colour once the engine's gaps were closed), so a rebuild that looks plainer than its reference is plainer in its scene script. Start from the diorama closest to the place and lift its builders as they are — its land and water, its buildings, its vegetation, its small life — then fit them to the map. Don't hand-write a simpler version of something a diorama already does. The Look Book's scenes also had life drawn live over them (people strolling, ducks on the pond, birds crossing, a dawn mist on the water, fireflies): a place's world hooks can bring that too.

**Re-render or rebuild?** Re-running a place's scene script gives it the toy finish and live light on its existing models — quick, and worth doing everywhere. Making it look like the Look Book means *rebuilding* it: the land shaped, the vegetation layered, its houses from `assets_house.py`, small life added (steps 2-5). When rebuilding a place that's in the game, keep it working: every interaction, door, spawn, NPC actor and quest label where the game expects it (`apply_world` moves them onto what you build; keep labels stable or quests stop matching), what people walk on walkable — check with `python3 tools/bridge-art/audit_worlds.py`.

### The game menu: backpack, map, journal, fish, collection, ledger (`js/menu.js`)
Added after Beck found the game hard to follow ("it's hard to understand what's going on in a lot of places, where things are"): one window, Stardew's game menu, with folder tabs across the top — **Backpack**, **Map**, **Journal**, **Fish**, **Collection** — each tab a page its module registers (`BridgeMenu.page(id, { render, key, leave, resize })`). **Tab** opens it where you left it, **I** the Backpack, **J** the Journal, **N** the Map (M is the sound); the same key again or **Esc** closes it, Tab / Shift-Tab step through the tabs. On touch the **Menu pill** (it was the Journal pill, top left; it still pings when something's logged) opens it, and on a phone it fills the screen with the tabs as a row of icons. It's dressed like the world's dialogs — parchment in Lumar, neon in Arcadia, holo aboard, a CRT on Enigma, violet glass at the Singularity, frost on Hoarfell, linen on Verdance, green velvet edged in gold in Sylvara (`gm-sylvan`) — every colour a CSS variable (`--gm-*`) the theme sets on `#game-menu`. While it's open the world waits: controls off, hostiles paused (`BridgeInventory.isMenuOpen`), every key goes to the menu (the active page first), the hotbar and prompts hidden (`html.menu-open`).
- **Backpack** (`js/inventory.js` mounts its screen here): hotbar and pack as a grid, and under them **Quest items** (what a quest has given you to hand on, js/keyitems.js); the picked item described with what it's worth and who pays best (`BridgeStorage.worthLine`: the best specialist from people.json's `sell` topics, any trader, the Chandler), and what you can do with it — Use, Split (half into a free slot), Sell 1 / Sell all (when you're standing at a trader, the Chandler or someone who buys it: `BridgeStorage.buyerHere`), Send to the ship (the sea chest aboard) or Into the chest (one that's open), Drop (lands at your feet as loot that waits till you step away: `BridgeLoot.spawn(x, y, id, n, { dropped: true })`) and Throw away (asks twice). Click (tap) an item then a slot to move it, or drag it (a finger held a moment on touch); Sort by kind / Sort by worth (the pack, not the hotbar) and Stack like with like; what all you carry is worth; the next pack up and where it's sold (`BridgeStorage.nextPack`); health, energy and the wardrobe. A weapon (or a tool you can fight with: pickaxes, the ice axe) shows its **stats card** in place of the old one-line effects (`BridgeArms.card`, see *Fighting*): damage as a number and a bar, speed (Very quick … Very slow, with pips), reach, its kind and what its swings do, each trait as a pixel icon and a line in plain words, and how it compares with what you're holding (`+12 / −6 a hit · a second`). The same card is in every shop that sells weapons (the vendor dialog, choosers with a `stats` row — the Peddler — and a menu dialog's `item`: Hob's forge), the hotbar's hover card (`#ws-tip`) and the Collection; it takes its colours from the dialog theme it sits in (`.wstat`, `--ws-fill` / `--ws-good` / `--ws-bad` per `.wd-<theme>`, the `--gm-*` colours in the menu).
